I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you had little or no interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… during this 15-month period, yes. That’s probably the clearest part of what’s been going on.\n\nIt wasn’t like one dramatic day where I woke up and everything was gone, but there were definitely stretches — more than two weeks — where almost every day the things that usually make me feel like myself just felt flat. Photography walks are a good example because that’s usually something I don’t have to force. I’ll notice the light on a building or some little detail on a sidewalk and want to go out, but during that period I just… didn’t initiate it. It felt heavy, like another task on the list instead of something that gives me a little spark.\n\nAnd mentoring the kids, too. That normally matters to me a lot, and I still knew it mattered, but I didn’t feel that pull to reach out or follow up the way I usually do. Even keeping a clean calendar — which sounds minor, I know — usually gives me this sense of order, like I’m on top of my life. But it started feeling burdensome instead of satisfying.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely periods of at least a couple weeks where interest and pleasure were low nearly every day. That’s really what made me feel like something was off enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Is the family stress you mentioned still going on right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “still going on” in the same way. Some of it is kind of just… in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not like there’s one active crisis every day that I can point to and say, “That’s the stress.” But I also wouldn’t say it’s completely resolved and no longer affecting me.\n\nI guess I’d say there are still family-related things that feel unsettled, but I’m not sure how much they’re actively driving what I’m feeling right now versus just being part of the overall noise in my life. Sometimes I only realize it’s weighing on me after the fact, like I’ll be more tense or preoccupied and then think, “Oh, maybe that conversation or situation is still sitting with me.”\n\nSo… yes and no. It’s not as immediate as it may have been at certain points, but I don’t think it’s gone either.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past 15 months… I still don’t feel like I can say yes to that confidently. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was less enthusiastic or kind of pulled back from things, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as depressed or losing interest nearly every day for two weeks. I wasn’t really tracking it that way, and I don’t want to make it sound more definite than it was.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that this felt different enough for me to notice it. The things that usually make me feel like myself — going out for photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean and organized — started feeling flat and heavy. Like they weren’t giving me that normal sense of satisfaction, and I just stopped initiating them. Not because I decided they didn’t matter, but because they felt like effort in a way they usually don’t.\n\nSo for before this period, I’d say I’m not sure. For this more recent period, yes, that loss of interest was clear.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when the family situation feels relatively calm, do you still feel this loss of interest?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually part of what made it feel like it wasn’t just about the family situation.\n\nEven on days where nothing especially bad is happening, or things are relatively quiet, I still find myself not reaching for the things I normally would. Like, if the house is calm or I don’t have some stressful conversation hanging over me, you’d think I’d want to go take photos or get caught up with mentoring or straighten out my calendar and feel a little better from it. But a lot of the time it still feels flat. Heavy, almost.\n\nIt’s not that I decided those things don’t matter anymore. I still know they matter to me. I can explain why they matter. I just don’t initiate them the way I used to, and when I think about doing them, it feels more like pushing myself through a task than being drawn toward something I enjoy. So yes, even when the family stuff is quieter, that loss of interest is still there.\n\nDoctor: During this past 15-month period, have you felt sad or down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. I know there have been stretches where I felt low, or kind of emotionally weighed down, but “sad or down nearly every day for at least two weeks” is harder for me to pin down.\n\nPart of the problem is that I don’t always experience it as obvious sadness, like crying or thinking, “I’m depressed today.” Sometimes it’s more like I’m moving through the day with less color in it, if that makes sense. But whether that was nearly every day for two full weeks… I honestly don’t know. I wasn’t marking the days, and I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like it might fit.\n\nI can say there were definitely periods during these 15 months where I felt off and lower than normal, but I’m not confident about the frequency or the exact duration in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, have you had a significant change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, not in a way where I could say, “My appetite clearly went up,” or “I stopped eating,” or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I eat more casually or I don’t pay much attention, but that’s not unusual for me depending on how busy the day is. And there are days where food just feels kind of like something I have to fit in, but I wouldn’t say my appetite itself changed dramatically. I haven’t had a clear pattern where I’m much hungrier than usual or not hungry for long stretches.\n\nSo I’d probably say no, or at least not that I’ve noticed in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have had trouble sleeping, though it’s a little strange because it isn’t always the kind where I’m lying there miserable all night.\n\nThere have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I wake up before dawn — like really early — and instead of feeling exhausted the way I would expect, I feel like I could just get up and start doing things. I’ll think about answering emails or updating appointment lists, checking details, organizing something work-related, that kind of thing. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Oh no, I can’t sleep,” exactly. Sometimes it feels more like my mind is already switched on and ready to go.\n\nIt’s noticeable because normally if I only slept that little, I’d assume I’d be dragging, but on those nights I don’t always feel tired right away. I just get this early-morning readiness, almost like the day has started before it should have. So yes, during this period there have definitely been nights like that.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only two or three hours and woke up ready to go, did you also feel unusually good, energized, or more confident than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single time, but there are definitely stretches where I feel unusually upbeat, like things are just clicking into place in a way they normally don’t. I’ll wake up early after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, I know exactly what needs to be done, I can handle all of it, everything is organized in my head.\n\nAnd I do feel more confident during those times, probably more than is typical for me. It’s not always some huge dramatic feeling, but it’s like I’m more sure of myself, like I don’t second-guess every little thing. I’ll feel very on top of things.\n\nBut the other side of it is that I can get sharp really quickly. Like if I’m in that mode and someone changes a meeting time or moves something around, even something small, I can react with more edge than I mean to. I’ll hear myself being short with someone and think afterward, “Why did I say it like that?” It surprises me because in the moment it feels like they’ve disrupted the whole flow, even if objectively it’s just a minor change. So yes, there’s that upbeat, energized, confident part, but it can flip into irritability pretty fast over little things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, they do. I mean, not like every single week on a perfect schedule, but those stretches where I’m sleeping only a couple hours and still feel really driven or overly confident — they can go on for several days in a row, yes. Four days would definitely fit some of them.

And it’s been something that keeps coming back. I’d say I’ve been having those elevated, kind of pushed-forward periods for maybe around eleven months now. It’s not just one isolated weekend where I stayed up too late or had too much coffee or something. It’s more like these recurring runs where I don’t seem to need much sleep, and I feel unusually ready to take things on, like I can handle more than usual. Then after a while it settles down, but it comes back again.

It’s not always some huge dramatic feeling, but it’s like I’m more sure of myself, like I don’t second-guess every little thing. I’ll feel very on top of things.\n\nBut the other side of it is that I can get sharp really quickly. Like if I’m in that mode and someone changes a meeting time or moves something around, even something small, I can react with more edge than I mean to. I’ll hear myself being short with someone and think afterward, “Why did I say it like that?” It surprises me because in the moment it feels like they’ve disrupted the whole flow, even if objectively it’s just a minor change. So yes, there’s that upbeat, energized, confident part, but it can flip into irritability pretty fast over little things.\n\nDoctor: When you have those periods of needing only two or three hours of sleep and feeling unusually energized, do they last at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I mean, not like every single week on a perfect schedule, but those stretches where I’m sleeping only a couple hours and still feel really driven or overly confident — they can go on for several days in a row, yes. Four days would definitely fit some of them.\n\nAnd it’s been something that keeps coming back. I’d say I’ve been having those elevated, kind of pushed-forward periods for maybe around eleven months now. It’s not just one isolated weekend where I stayed up too late or had too much coffee or something. It’s more like these recurring runs where I don’t seem to need much sleep, and I feel unusually ready to take things on, like I can handle more than usual. Then after a while it settles down, but it comes back again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those energized periods, do you find yourself starting a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Activity\nmanifestation: \"During those stretches I start rebuilding work templates, making care plans, emailing relatives, and reorganizing files all at once, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. During those stretches I sort of… I don’t just do one thing, I start opening up everything at once.

Like at work, I’ll decide the templates we use need to be rebuilt or cleaned up, so I’ll start making new versions of those. Then while I’m doing that, I’ll think, well, the files are a mess too, so I’ll start reorganizing folders and renaming things and making lists of what needs updating. And then I’ll remember care plans or appointment-related stuff that could be clearer, so I’ll start working on that too.

It isn’t just work either. I’ll start emailing relatives, trying to coordinate things or explain plans or check in on people, and it feels very urgent in the moment, like if I don’t handle it right then, I’m somehow dropping the ball. I can have several things going at the same time — templates, care plans, emails, files — and I’ll feel like sitting still would be irresponsible, almost. Like I need to keep moving from one task to the next because everything suddenly seems important and fixable.

I mean, I’ve had low stretches, definitely, and lately it’s felt more noticeable, but when you say “most days” over two years, that feels hard for me to answer cleanly.\n\nI tend to think in terms of what was going on around me at the time, so I can remember periods where I was just kind of getting through the day and not feeling great, but I don’t know that I was down or low most days for that whole span. I’d have to really look back month by month to say that, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you felt sad or down most of the day on more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, this past year has definitely had a heavier feeling to it at times, and I’ve had days where I felt kind of low or worn down emotionally. But “most of the day” and “more days than not” — that’s where I get stuck, because I don’t know if I’ve been tracking it that clearly.\n\nSome days I’d feel down in the morning and then get pulled into work or responsibilities and not notice it as much. Other days I might feel okay for a while and then it would hit me later. So I don’t want to say yes if that means it was clearly there most of the day, most days. I’d say… I’ve had sadness or low mood during the year, but whether it meets that exact pattern, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest or low mood for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past year… I mean, I’ve had times where I was low before, but the thing that really made me come in now is more this feeling that the things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat. Like, I usually like going on photography walks — not in some grand artistic way, just walking around noticing light and buildings and little details — and I stopped initiating that. It started to feel heavy, like another obligation instead of something that gave me something back.\n\nAnd mentoring the kids, too… that’s usually important to me. I’m usually the person who follows up, checks in, helps them think through stuff. But lately even that felt kind of muted, like I cared in principle but couldn’t really get myself to start. Even keeping a clean calendar — which sounds small, but for me that’s part of feeling organized and like myself — started feeling strangely burdensome instead of satisfying.\n\nSo, if you mean before this past year, another clear two-week stretch exactly like this… I’m not sure. I’ve had low periods, yes, but this particular flatness and not starting the things I normally care about is what’s been standing out more recently.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart, because it hasn’t. I’m still getting to work, and from the outside I probably look pretty functional. My work has stayed intact overall.\n\nBut I do notice these little slips that bother me because they’re not really like me. I work with calendars and details, so normally certain things are almost automatic — checking times, confirming appointments, keeping track of follow-ups. Lately I’ve missed a few calendar details, or I’ll catch myself needing to double-check something I normally would’ve just handled without thinking. It’s not catastrophic, but it makes me feel less steady, like I can’t fully trust my usual rhythm.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back more. Photography walks used to be something I’d just do naturally, and I haven’t really been initiating those. With mentoring, too, I still care about it, but I don’t feel as present or reliable as I usually would, so I’ve kind of stepped back instead of pushing myself and then not following through. So yes, it has made daily life harder, just in a quieter way rather than a total collapse.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15-month period, was there ever a time in your life when you felt this same kind of loss of interest or low mood nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that clearly. I’ve definitely had periods before this where I felt low or less engaged, but when you say “nearly every day for at least two weeks,” I start second-guessing it, because I don’t think I was measuring it that way at the time.\n\nThere were times in the past where I’d get withdrawn or feel like I was just going through the motions, but I can’t confidently say it was the same kind of loss of interest, nearly every day, for a full two weeks or more. This more recent stretch is the one I can describe better because it’s been more noticeable to me — like the things I usually enjoy or keep up with started feeling harder to initiate.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, was there ever a stretch of at least two weeks when you had little or no interest or pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… during this 15-month period, yes. That’s probably the clearest part of what’s been going on.\n\nIt wasn’t like one dramatic day where I woke up and everything was gone, but there were definitely stretches — more than two weeks — where almost every day the things that usually make me feel like myself just felt flat. Photography walks are a good example because that’s usually something I don’t have to force. I’ll notice the light on a building or some little detail on a sidewalk and want to go out, but during that period I just… didn’t initiate it. It felt heavy, like another task on the list instead of something that gives me a little spark.\n\nAnd mentoring the kids, too. That normally matters to me a lot, and I still knew it mattered, but I didn’t feel that pull to reach out or follow up the way I usually do. Even keeping a clean calendar — which sounds minor, I know — usually gives me this sense of order, like I’m on top of my life. But it started feeling burdensome instead of satisfying.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely periods of at least a couple weeks where interest and pleasure were low nearly every day. That’s really what made me feel like something was off enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Is the family stress you mentioned still going on right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “still going on” in the same way. Some of it is kind of just… in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not like there’s one active crisis every day that I can point to and say, “That’s the stress.” But I also wouldn’t say it’s completely resolved and no longer affecting me.\n\nI guess I’d say there are still family-related things that feel unsettled, but I’m not sure how much they’re actively driving what I’m feeling right now versus just being part of the overall noise in my life. Sometimes I only realize it’s weighing on me after the fact, like I’ll be more tense or preoccupied and then think, “Oh, maybe that conversation or situation is still sitting with me.”\n\nSo… yes and no. It’s not as immediate as it may have been at certain points, but I don’t think it’s gone either.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past 15 months… I still don’t feel like I can say yes to that confidently. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was less enthusiastic or kind of pulled back from things, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as depressed or losing interest nearly every day for two weeks. I wasn’t really tracking it that way, and I don’t want to make it sound more definite than it was.\n\nWhat made me come in now is that this felt different enough for me to notice it. The things that usually make me feel like myself — going out for photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean and organized — started feeling flat and heavy. Like they weren’t giving me that normal sense of satisfaction, and I just stopped initiating them. Not because I decided they didn’t matter, but because they felt like effort in a way they usually don’t.\n\nSo for before this period, I’d say I’m not sure. For this more recent period, yes, that loss of interest was clear.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when the family situation feels relatively calm, do you still feel this loss of interest?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that’s actually part of what made it feel like it wasn’t just about the family situation.\n\nEven on days where nothing especially bad is happening, or things are relatively quiet, I still find myself not reaching for the things I normally would. Like, if the house is calm or I don’t have some stressful conversation hanging over me, you’d think I’d want to go take photos or get caught up with mentoring or straighten out my calendar and feel a little better from it. But a lot of the time it still feels flat. Heavy, almost.\n\nIt’s not that I decided those things don’t matter anymore. I still know they matter to me. I can explain why they matter. I just don’t initiate them the way I used to, and when I think about doing them, it feels more like pushing myself through a task than being drawn toward something I enjoy. So yes, even when the family stuff is quieter, that loss of interest is still there.\n\nDoctor: During this past 15-month period, have you felt sad or down nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. I know there have been stretches where I felt low, or kind of emotionally weighed down, but “sad or down nearly every day for at least two weeks” is harder for me to pin down.\n\nPart of the problem is that I don’t always experience it as obvious sadness, like crying or thinking, “I’m depressed today.” Sometimes it’s more like I’m moving through the day with less color in it, if that makes sense. But whether that was nearly every day for two full weeks… I honestly don’t know. I wasn’t marking the days, and I don’t want to just say yes because it sounds like it might fit.\n\nI can say there were definitely periods during these 15 months where I felt off and lower than normal, but I’m not confident about the frequency or the exact duration in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, have you had a significant change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, not in a way where I could say, “My appetite clearly went up,” or “I stopped eating,” or anything like that.\n\nThere are days where I eat more casually or I don’t pay much attention, but that’s not unusual for me depending on how busy the day is. And there are days where food just feels kind of like something I have to fit in, but I wouldn’t say my appetite itself changed dramatically. I haven’t had a clear pattern where I’m much hungrier than usual or not hungry for long stretches.\n\nSo I’d probably say no, or at least not that I’ve noticed in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: During this 15-month period, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have had trouble sleeping, though it’s a little strange because it isn’t always the kind where I’m lying there miserable all night.\n\nThere have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I wake up before dawn — like really early — and instead of feeling exhausted the way I would expect, I feel like I could just get up and start doing things. I’ll think about answering emails or updating appointment lists, checking details, organizing something work-related, that kind of thing. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Oh no, I can’t sleep,” exactly. Sometimes it feels more like my mind is already switched on and ready to go.\n\nIt’s noticeable because normally if I only slept that little, I’d assume I’d be dragging, but on those nights I don’t always feel tired right away. I just get this early-morning readiness, almost like the day has started before it should have. So yes, during this period there have definitely been nights like that.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only two or three hours and woke up ready to go, did you also feel unusually good, energized, or more confident than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single time, but there are definitely stretches where I feel unusually upbeat, like things are just clicking into place in a way they normally don’t. I’ll wake up early after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, I know exactly what needs to be done, I can handle all of it, everything is organized in my head.\n\nAnd I do feel more confident during those times, probably more than is typical for me. It’s not always some huge dramatic feeling, but it’s like I’m more sure of myself, like I don’t second-guess every little thing. I’ll feel very on top of things.\n\nBut the other side of it is that I can get sharp really quickly. Like if I’m in that mode and someone changes a meeting time or moves something around, even something small, I can react with more edge than I mean to. I’ll hear myself being short with someone and think afterward, “Why did I say it like that?” It surprises me because in the moment it feels like they’ve disrupted the whole flow, even if objectively it’s just a minor change. So yes, there’s that upbeat, energized, confident part, but it can flip into irritability pretty fast over little things.\n\nDoctor: When you have those periods of needing only two or three hours of sleep and feeling unusually energized, do they last at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I mean, not like every single week on a perfect schedule, but those stretches where I’m sleeping only a couple hours and still feel really driven or overly confident — they can go on for several days in a row, yes. Four days would definitely fit some of them.\n\nAnd it’s been something that keeps coming back. I’d say I’ve been having those elevated, kind of pushed-forward periods for maybe around eleven months now. It’s not just one isolated weekend where I stayed up too late or had too much coffee or something. It’s more like these recurring runs where I don’t seem to need much sleep, and I feel unusually ready to take things on, like I can handle more than usual. Then after a while it settles down, but it comes back again.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you find yourself starting a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. During those stretches I sort of… I don’t just do one thing, I start opening up everything at once.\n\nLike at work, I’ll decide the templates we use need to be rebuilt or cleaned up, so I’ll start making new versions of those. Then while I’m doing that, I’ll think, well, the files are a mess too, so I’ll start reorganizing folders and renaming things and making lists of what needs updating. And then I’ll remember care plans or appointment-related stuff that could be clearer, so I’ll start working on that too.\n\nIt isn’t just work either. I’ll start emailing relatives, trying to coordinate things or explain plans or check in on people, and it feels very urgent in the moment, like if I don’t handle it right then, I’m somehow dropping the ball. I can have several things going at the same time — templates, care plans, emails, files — and I’ll feel like sitting still would be irresponsible, almost. Like I need to keep moving from one task to the next because everything suddenly seems important and fixable."
